    Where this goes next

    A matrix is a rule for moving vectors, and Wx + b is a layer: a linear rule plus an offset. Composition makes those rules stack - the architecture of every network in this book. Next we stop treating the map as fixed and ask how to change it to make it better - which means slopes and derivatives.

    Chapter 04 - Functions, Derivatives, and Slopes

    Back to Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/00 - Book Index.

    04 · Functions, Derivatives, and Slopes

    Part I - The Mathematics

    04 - Functions, Derivatives, and Slopes

    How to use this chapter

    Read it, build the three functions in labkit/calculus.py, run the checks, answer the questions at the end out loud, then tell me what landed and what was thin. The code is yours. I scaffolded the files; I did not write the bodies.

    Build target: labkit/calculus.py (3 functions), labkit/tests/test_calculus.py (8 checks).

    Chapter 01 gave you scalars and shape rules: Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/01 - vectors-and-shapes. Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/02 - dot-products and Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/03 - matrices-as-maps gave you the operations on them. This chapter adds one idea: how fast a number changes when the number you feed in changes.

    Why this matters

    A trained neural network is, underneath, a pile of numbers. Change one of them - a weight - and the model's output changes. The question driving every training algorithm ever written is: if I nudge this number up, does the loss go up or down, and by how much?

    That "by how much" is a derivative. The loss is a function of the parameters, and the derivative of the loss with respect to one parameter tells you the direction and steepness of the local slope. Move a little the way the derivative points, repeat a few million times, and the model improves. That is the engine of training. Everything in Part III - backpropagation, gradient descent, optimizers - is this one idea applied more widely.

    You could learn gradients purely from code and never touch calculus, but you would also be unable to debug the code: almost every numerical bug in a training loop is a bug about slope accuracy.

    The naive story

    Here is the story most people arrive with. Calculus is about infinitely small quantities. A derivative is a fraction with a zero on top and a zero on the bottom, so it must be undefined. It is the slope you would get if the interval shrank all the way to zero - and since you can never actually shrink an interval to zero, you can never evaluate a derivative, only approach it. Derivatives belong to pure theory; if you want a number, you must solve it symbolically.

    There is a second, vaguer half: "the slope of a curve." But a curve does not have one slope. If the answer depends on where you look, which number is the slope?

    Both halves are wrong in a useful direction. The zero-over-zero fraction is not undefined; it is a precise question with a precise answer. And the derivative is computable in code, to six significant digits, with two subtractions and one division. The trick is that you never shrink the interval to zero - you shrink it to a small-but-sane number and average the two sides.

    The real thing

    A function is a machine. It takes numbers in and hands a number back. f(x) = 3x + 1 takes 3 and returns 10, takes -2 and returns -5. One input, one output, the same output every time. That is the whole definition.

    The slope answers "if I move the input a little, how much does the output move?" For a straight line the answer is the same everywhere - that is what makes a straight line straight. f(x) = 3x + 1 rises 3 for every 1 you step right, at x = -100 and at x = +100 alike. For a curve the answer changes with x, so the question becomes "what is the slope right here?"

    The derivative is that local answer, made precise. If f'(x) is the derivative at x, then for a small h:

    f(x + h) ≈ f(x) + f'(x) * h

    The output at x+h is approximately the output at x, plus the slope times how far you moved. The tangent line is the best straight line lying against the curve at x, and this equation is that line reduced to one line of arithmetic. That is what "best local linear approximation" buys you: a formula you can run.

    Computing it without shrinking to zero. Take the slope of the straight line through the two points x-h and x+h on the curve:

    derivative(f, x, h) = (f(x + h) - f(x - h)) / (2 * h)

    Why two-sided rather than the one-sided (f(x+h) - f(x))/h? Symmetry. The forward and backward differences each carry an error proportional to h, one biased high and the other biased low. Average them and the bias cancels: the central difference's error falls to order h² instead of order h.

    You can see this with no theory, on a quadratic. For f(x) = x² at x = 3 with h = 1e-3, the forward difference gives (9.006001 - 9) / 0.001 = 6.001 - wrong in the third decimal. The central difference at the same h gives (9.006001 - 8.994001) / 0.002 = 0.012 / 0.002 = 6.000 - exactly right. Same step, ten times better. That is why derivative() uses the central difference.

    Choosing h. Two errors pull in opposite directions.

    Too large: you average over too much bending. For f(x) = x³ at x = 3 with h = 1, (f(4) - f(2)) / 2 = (64 - 8) / 2 = 28. The true derivative is 27 - wrong by exactly h², because for a cubic the central difference works out to 3x² + h².

    Too small: floating-point noise takes over. A Python float keeps about 15-16 significant digits. Near x = 3 the values of f are around 9, so each evaluation carries rounding noise of roughly 2e-15. You then divide by 2h, multiplying that noise by 1/(2h):

    h noise multiplied by noise in the answer, near f(x) = 9
    1e-3 5e2 about 1e-12
    1e-7 5e6 about 1e-8
    1e-12 5e11 about 1e-3 - visible
    1e-16 5e15 total garbage

    So the sweet spot sits in the middle. h = 1e-7 works well for doubles around x ~ 1, and h = 1e-4 is fine for smooth functions near x ~ 3. A step of h = 1e-3 is too coarse for f(x) = x² at x = 3 - the forward difference already gives 6.001, third decimal wrong - and the central difference escapes it only because a quadratic's bending terms cancel exactly; on x³ the same h gives 27.000001. And h = 1e-12 gives garbage.

    Worked examples

    Example 1: f(x) = 3x + 1. Slope from two points on the graph: x = 1 gives y = 4, x = 4 gives y = 13.

    (13 - 4) / (4 - 1) = 9 / 3 = 3

    Now the central difference at x = 2 with h = 1e-7. By hand, f(2+h) = 7 + 3h and f(2-h) = 7 - 3h, so the numerator is 6h and the answer is 6h / 2h = 3. In Python this returns 2.9999999995311555 - off by 5e-10, exactly the h = 1e-7 noise floor above. Both routes land on 3; the gap between them tells you how much of the number is real.

    Example 2: f(x) = x² at x = 3, with h = 1e-4. Expand both squares:

    (3 + 1e-4)² = 9 + 6(1e-4) + (1e-4)² = 9.00060001
(3 - 1e-4)² = 9 - 6(1e-4) + (1e-4)² = 8.99940001
difference = 0.00120000
2h = 0.0002
0.0012 / 0.0002 = 6

    The true derivative of x² is 2x, so at x = 3 it is 6 - the numeric result matches exactly. Notice why: the two h² terms are identical and cancel. Expand (3+h)² - (3-h)² and you get 12h, so the answer is 12h / 2h = 6 for every h. The central difference of a quadratic is exact. Python returns 6.000000000012662; the extra 1.3e-11 is rounding, not method error.

    Example 3: numeric versus analytic, and one badly chosen h. With h = 1e-7 throughout:

    f x analytic f' numeric
    x² 0 0 0.0000000000
    x² 1 2 2.0000000001
    x² 3 6 5.9999999902
    x² -2 -4 -3.9999999957
    x³ 1 3 3.0000000001
    x³ 3 27 26.9999999603
    x³ -2 12 11.9999999937

    Every numeric entry is within 4e-8 of its analytic value. Now the badly chosen rows:

    f x h numeric true what went wrong
    x³ 3 1 28.0 27 h² = 1 of bending; step far too large
    x² 3 1e-12 6.000533 6 noise multiplied by 5e11
    x² 3 1e-16 0.0 6 noise multiplied by 5e15; total garbage

    A step that is too large reports a slope averaged over too much curve; too small reports rounding noise amplified by a huge factor. Neither is a bug in your code.

    Why this matters for AI. Take a model with one weight w. Its loss L(w) is a function: feed in a weight, get back a loss. The derivative L'(w) says which way to move that weight to make the loss smaller, and how big a move is worth taking. Negative derivative - move w up. Positive derivative - move w down. That is the whole engine of training. In Chapter 05 a real model has thousands of weights, so you need a slope in thousands of directions at once; that object is the gradient, and it is a vector. In Chapter 06 the chain rule finds those slopes inside a network without differentiating by hand.

    Where this sits in the code

    A new module, labkit/calculus.py. Three functions in this chapter, with the exact names and signatures from labkit/CONTRACT.md:

    Function Signature Behaviour
    derivative derivative(f, x: float, h: float = 1e-7) -> float central difference (f(x+h) - f(x-h)) / (2h)
    square square(x: float) -> float x * x
    cube cube(x: float) -> float x * x * x

    f is any one-argument callable taking a float and returning a float. derivative always returns a float. The default h is 1e-7.

    Cost: derivative calls f twice per evaluation - once at x+h, once at x-h. One numeric derivative costs two calls of f. For square and cube that is nothing. For a model's loss, where one evaluation can cost milliseconds and the model has millions of parameters, it is hopeless - which is why Chapter 19 builds automatic differentiation instead of sampling the loss. For now: numeric differentiation is a measurement tool, not a production method.

    The later contract functions - numeric_gradient, compose, chain_grad - are built on top of derivative. Tests go in labkit/tests/test_calculus.py.

    Your turn - build it

    File 1: labkit/calculus.py

    Three functions, exactly as in the contract table above: derivative(f, x, h=1e-7) evaluates f at x+h and x-h and returns (f(x+h) - f(x-h)) / (2*h) as a float; square(x) returns x * x; cube(x) returns x * x * x.

    File 2: labkit/tests/test_calculus.py

    Eight checks. The expected values are given; you write the assertions.

    1. square(3) == 9, square(-2) == 4, cube(3) == 27, cube(-2) == -8
    2. derivative(square, 3) is 6 to within 1e-5
    3. derivative(cube, 3) is 27 to within 1e-5
    4. derivative(square, -2) is -4 to within 1e-5
    5. derivative(cube, -2) is 12 to within 1e-5
    6. derivative(square, 0) and derivative(cube, 0) are both 0 to within 1e-5
    7. derivative calls f exactly twice: define a function that counts its own calls, call derivative on it once, assert the count is 2
    8. the bad-h case: derivative(square, 3, h=1e-16) is not close to 6 - it comes out at 0.0. Assert it is not within 1e-5 of 6

    On tolerance. You cannot use == on a computed derivative, and this chapter is the reason: the answer carries rounding error that depends on h (about 4e-8 at h = 1e-7 above). So compare with a margin: unittest's assertAlmostEqual(a, b, delta=1e-5) and assertNotAlmostEqual for check 8. Why 1e-5: the worst deviation in checks 2-6 is 4e-8, so 1e-5 passes them with three orders of magnitude to spare, while the bad-h row from Example 3 was off by 5.3e-4 - which fails at 1e-5. A tolerance of 1.0 would pass everything and teach you nothing; 1e-12 would fail on rounding alone.

    Run

    From C:\Users\aswat\Projects\laya-try:

    python -m unittest discover -s labkit/tests -v

    Eight new tests, and the six from Chapter 01 still pass. If a test errors with NotImplementedError, your body is still the scaffold's.

    Hint: the derivative is the slope of the line through two points on the curve - take the points x-h and x+h, divide the height difference by the width difference. Everything else (which h, how many calls of f) follows from that picture.

    Check yourself

    Answer these out loud, in your own words, before Chapter 05.

    1. For f(x) = 3x + 1, the slope from the points (1, 4) and (4, 13) is 3, and the central difference at x = 2 with h = 1e-7 is 2.9999999995311555. Why are these two numbers not exactly equal, and which is "more correct"?
    2. Expand (3+h)² - (3-h)² by hand. What does the cancellation of the h² terms tell you about how the central difference behaves on any quadratic, at any step size?
    3. Your numeric derivative of x² at x = 3 came out as 6.000533 instead of 6. Is that an arithmetic mistake or a choice-of-h problem? Name the two competing sources of error, and say which side of the sweet spot each comes from.
    4. For f(x) = x³ at x = 3 with h = 1, the central difference gives 28 but the true derivative is 27. Expand (3+h)³ - (3-h)³ and show the central difference of a cubic at x is exactly 3x² + h². Then: is 28 a bug, or the formula working correctly with a bad h?
    5. derivative calls f twice. If evaluating a model's loss took 0.4 seconds, how long would one numeric derivative of that loss take? Why does that make numeric differentiation unusable for a million-parameter model, and what does that tell you to build instead?

    Where this goes next

    This chapter gave you one input, one output, one slope. A real model has thousands of parameters, so its loss is a function of thousands of numbers at once and you need a slope in every direction simultaneously - that object is the gradient, it is a vector, and it is the subject of the next chapter. The chain rule after that finds those slopes inside a network without differentiating by hand.

    Chapter 05 - Partial Derivatives and the Gradient

    05 · Partial Derivatives and the Gradient

    Part I - The Mathematics

    05 - Partial Derivatives and the Gradient

    How to use this chapter

    Read it once straight through, then open labkit/calculus.py, write the one function it asks for, and run the checks. Answer the five questions at the end out loud before you read Chapter 06.

    You need the dot product and norm from Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/01 - vectors-and-shapes and the central difference from Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/04 - derivatives. That is all.

    Build target: labkit/calculus.py (1 new function), labkit/tests/test_calculus.py (6 checks).

    Why this matters

    A neural network has millions of numbers, and every one is a knob. The loss is a single number saying how bad the model is right now - so it is a function of all those numbers at once.

    To learn, you need to know, for every knob at the same time, which way to turn it and how hard. That is what the gradient is: one number per knob. A model with 70 million parameters gets a gradient with 70 million numbers - one vector subtraction moves every knob.

    The naive story

    The naive story: a derivative tells you the slope; more variables, more slopes; each slope is a separate independent thing about one variable, so you handle them one at a time. With a million parameters, do that a million times.

    It is wrong twice. First, it treats the slopes as separate: they all come off the same surface at the same point, so turn one knob and the slope with respect to another changes too. Second, it treats the gradient as a pile of numbers. It is a single arrow, drawn in the space of all inputs at once, pointing one way.

    The real thing

    Take one function with many inputs:

    f(x, y) = x^2 + 3y^2

    A bowl: floor at (0, 0), deeper in y than in x because of the 3. Every point in the input space (the plane) has a height f(x, y).

    Partial derivatives: the slope with one thing frozen

    To find the slope in the x direction at a point, do the Chapter 04 thing with x as the variable and y held fixed: freeze y where it is, slide along x, measure the slope of the height. That is the partial derivative with respect to x: we ask about one coordinate and ignore the others.

    With y frozen the 3y^2 part does not move, so it drops out and the slope is 2x. Freeze x instead and you get 6y:

    df/dx = 2x
df/dy = 6y

    The gradient: one number per input, packaged together

    Put the partials in a vector, in the order of the inputs:

    gradient of f = (df/dx, df/dy) = (2x, 6y)

    One rule matters more than any other: the gradient has the same shape as the input. Two inputs, two entries; a thousand parameters, a thousand entries. The i-th entry is the derivative with respect to the i-th input - which is why the code is uniform: parameters of length d, loss f(params), gradient of length d, subtract entry by entry.

    The gradient points uphill

    The most useful sentence in this chapter: the gradient points in the direction of steepest ascent. Steepest descent is its negative, -gradient. The proof:

    Moving a small amount in some direction h changes the value by approximately the dot product of the gradient with h:

    f(x + h)  ~=  f(x) + gradient(x) . h

    The ~= is the honest part: the true change also has terms that shrink faster than the step, negligible for tiny steps.

    Write g for the gradient at x and pick h = -t * g - move against the gradient, by an amount t:

    f(x - t*g)  ~=  f(x) + g . (-t*g)
             =  f(x) - t * (g . g)
             =  f(x) - t * ||g||^2

    g . g is the squared length of g, and it is strictly positive whenever g is not the zero vector. So for any small positive t, f(x - t*g) is smaller than f(x). Moving against the gradient goes downhill. QED.

    For "steepest": with a unit direction u, f(x + t*u) ~= f(x) + t*(g.u), and the dot product is at most the product of the lengths - maximal only when the vectors point the same way. So g.u <= ||g||, with equality only when u is the gradient, and the biggest possible increase comes from moving along g.

    For this quadratic the expansion is exact, not approximate:

    f(x - t*g) = f(x) - t * ||g||^2 + t^2 * (4x^2 + 108y^2)

    The first-order term is exactly -t*||g||^2; the t^2 term is the leftover, t times smaller for small t. At x = 1, y = 2, t = 0.1: 13 - 14.8 + 4.36 = 2.56, the number Worked Example 2 lands on.

    Why this is training

    The loss is a function of every parameter, the gradient says how to move every parameter at once, and params = params - learning_rate * gradient is the training step. Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/13 - training-loop is that step wrapped in a loop, run thousands of times - the same arithmetic, with a bigger d and a smaller learning rate.

    The geometry: contours and the perpendicular

    A contour is the set of points where f has the same value. The contour through (1, 2) is x^2 + 3y^2 = 13, an ellipse - a level path around the bowl where the height never changes.

    The gradient is perpendicular to the contour at that point. On the curve x^2 + 3y^2 is constant, so 2x dx + 6y dy = 0. At (1, 2): 2 dx + 12 dy = 0, so a tangent direction is (-6, 1). The gradient is (2, 12), and

    (2, 12) . (-6, 1) = 2*(-6) + 12*1 = -12 + 12 = 0

    Zero dot product means perpendicular. Contours are rings around the floor, and the gradient is a straight arrow at right angles to the ring at every point. Since the gradient points uphill and the contour is flat, it must be the direction of steepest climb - the proof above, seen from above.

    One thing this tells you: at (1, 2) the direction to the bottom is (-1, -2), but steepest descent is (-2, -12). The bowl is not round - it is deeper in y - so the steepest way down is not the straight way down; gradient descent curves toward the floor instead of aiming at it.

    The cost, stated plainly

    This chapter computes the gradient numerically. Each partial uses a central difference, which is two evaluations of f; with d inputs that is 2*d evaluations per gradient.

    For a model with 10 million parameters that is 20 million forward passes per gradient; over a thousand steps, twenty billion. It is hopeless - a real limitation of this approach, not a detail.

    Real frameworks compute the gradient analytically instead, applying differentiation rules to the model's own formula - that is Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/19 - backpropagation, where getting all d partials costs roughly one forward pass. The numeric method survives only because it is good for checking an analytic gradient: if the two disagree, the analytic one has a bug.

    Worked examples

    Example 1 - the gradient at a point.

    f(x, y) = x^2 + 3y^2 at (1, 2):

    df/dx = 2x = 2*1 = 2
df/dy = 6y = 6*2 = 12
gradient = (2, 12)

    Two inputs, two entries.

    Example 2 - one gradient descent step.

    Same function, same point, learning rate 0.1. Move against the gradient:

    x_new = 1 - 0.1 * 2  = 0.8
y_new = 2 - 0.1 * 12 = 0.8

    New point (0.8, 0.8). New value 0.64 + 1.92 = 2.56. The old value was 13, so the loss dropped from 13 to 2.56. Every training step in a real model is this: one dot product against the gradient, one subtraction per parameter.

    Example 3 - the same function at the floor.

    At (0, 0): df/dx = 0 and df/dy = 0, so the gradient is (0, 0). A step of size 0.1 in the direction -gradient moves the point by 0.1 * (0, 0) = (0, 0). Nothing happens. The algorithm has arrived and stops.

    This is a real failure mode of networks. If every weight starts at zero, every layer's output is the same regardless of input and the gradient with respect to those weights is zero - so the network never learns. Networks are therefore initialised with small random numbers instead of zeros: randomness breaks the symmetry so the gradient has something to point at. How that is done is in Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/20 - gradient-descent.

    Where this sits in the code

    One function goes into labkit/calculus.py, alongside what Chapter 04 put there:

    numeric_gradient(f, xs: list[float], h: float = 1e-7) -> list[float]

    Behaviour. f takes a list of floats (the same length as xs) and returns a float - for example f = lambda xs: xs[0]**2 + 3*xs[1]**2. The return value is a list of the same length as xs, where entry i is the derivative with respect to xs[i], every other entry held fixed.

    What to reuse. Chapter 04's derivative(f, x, h) already computes (f(x+h) - f(x-h)) / (2h) for a function of one variable. Each partial here is that, applied to a one-variable function built by freezing all but one coordinate. One difference formula, not two.

    Cost. 2 * d calls of f, where d = len(xs). Say this in the docstring; it is the whole reason Chapter 19 exists.

    Do not get this wrong. Do not modify xs in place - build a new list for each probe, or the second probe measures the wrong point. One entry per input, in the input's order.

    Tests go in labkit/tests/test_calculus.py, following the unittest pattern Chapter 04 used.

    Your turn - build it

    File: labkit/calculus.py

    Function: numeric_gradient(f, xs: list[float], h: float = 1e-7) -> list[float]

    Behaviour: returns one partial derivative per entry of xs, in order, with all other inputs held fixed. Does not mutate xs.

    Checks for labkit/tests/test_calculus.py (use assertAlmostEqual - numeric results are close to, not exactly, the true values):

    1. f = lambda xs: xs[0]**2 + 3*xs[1]**2 at [1.0, 2.0] gives [2.0, 12.0], each within 1e-5.
    2. The same f at [0.0, 0.0] gives [0.0, 0.0], each within 1e-5.
    3. f = lambda xs: 3*xs[0] - 2*xs[1] at [1.0, 1.0] gives [3.0, -2.0], each within 1e-5.
    4. numeric_gradient(f, [1.0, 2.0]) has length 2, and the input list [1.0, 2.0] is unchanged afterwards.
    5. f = lambda xs: xs[0]**2 at [3.0] gives [6.0] within 1e-5 - one input, one entry.
    6. A descent check: take g = numeric_gradient(f, [1.0, 2.0]), step to [xs[i] - 0.1*g[i] for i in range(2)], and assert the new value is smaller than 13.

    Run the checks from C:\Users\aswat\Projects\laya-try:

    python -m unittest discover -s labkit/tests -v

    Hint: think about the shape of the question before the arithmetic. Each partial derivative asks a one-variable question - "if this one coordinate moves and everything else stays put, how does the height change?" - and that is what derivative from Chapter 04 answers.

    Check yourself

    Answer these out loud, in your own words.

    1. For f(x, y) = x^2 + 3y^2, what are df/dx and df/dy at (3, -1), and what does "holding y fixed" mean while you compute df/dx?
    2. A model has 12 parameters and the gradient is a list of 12 numbers. What does the 7th number tell you, and what is the gradient's length?
    3. At (1, 2) the gradient is (2, 12) and steepest descent is (-2, -12), but the straight line to the bottom is (-1, -2). Why do those differ, and what does that say about the bowl?
    4. You take one gradient descent step from (1, 2) with learning rate 0.1. What are the new coordinates and the new value of f? Take a second step: what are the coordinates now, and did the value fall again?
    5. numeric_gradient needs 2*d calls of f for a function with d inputs. Why is that too slow for a million parameters, and what do real frameworks do instead?

    Where this goes next

    You now have a direction, not just a slope: one arrow per point in a space of any size, pointing uphill, with the training step expressible as a subtraction. What is missing is how to get that arrow when the function is itself built out of other functions - which is every model you will ever train.

    Chapter 06 - The Chain Rule

    06 · The Chain Rule

    Part I - The Mathematics

    06 - The Chain Rule

    How to use this chapter

    Read it with a pencil. Every number can be checked by hand in under a minute. Then build the two functions in labkit/calculus.py, run the checks, and answer the five questions at the end out loud.

    The chapter has one idea: when you plug one machine into another, the slope of the whole thing is the product of two slopes. Everything after that is the same idea applied again.

    You will need math.sin and math.cos, and the labkit/calculus.py file set up in Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/04 - derivatives.

    Build target: labkit/calculus.py (2 functions), labkit/tests/test_calculus.py (5 checks).

    Why this matters

    In Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/01 - vectors-and-shapes a layer was y = Wx + b: one matrix times one vector. That is one machine. A network with 40 layers is 40 of those stacked, and the last one feeds a single number called the loss.

    When a network learns it asks one question over and over: if I nudge this one weight, does the loss go up or down, and by how much? The answer is a slope.

    That question is hopeless for a weight in layer 1. Layer 1's output has to travel through 39 more layers before it reaches the loss, so the slope of the loss with respect to a layer-1 weight is the product of 40 slopes - one per machine on the path.

    The rule that lets you compute that product is the rule in this chapter, and it is why training works at all. Chapter 19 puts it in code; today we build the arithmetic and the intuition until it is obvious.

    The naive story

    Here is the story you are supposed to have in your head, and it is wrong.

    "f(g(x)) is one machine. Every machine has one slope. So the slope of f(g(x)) should be one number I can read off directly, the same way I read off the slope of square."

    Take a concrete pair: f(u) = 3u + 1 and g(x) = x^2. Together they give f(g(x)) = 3x^2 + 1, slope 6x, which is 12 at x = 2. Now apply the story: which derivative do you take? f'(x) = 3? The answer is 3. g'(x) = 2x = 4? The answer is 4.

    You cannot even find the number, and not because you are bad at arithmetic. The story assumes f(g(x)) is one machine, and it is not: it is two machines with a wire running from the output of one into the input of the other. Each has its own slope.

    And a slope is not a property of a machine in general. It is a property of a machine at a particular input. "The slope of f" is not a number until you say where. That second sentence is the whole chapter.

    The near-miss

    Most people do not stop there. They reach for "there are two slopes, so multiply them" and it almost works, which is worse than being wrong, because it feels right.

    Same setup, but f(u) = sin(u) and g(x) = x^2, at x = 2. The true slope is -2.6146.

    What you try What you get Right?
    Multiply f'(x) * g'(x) cos(2) * 4 = -1.6646 No
    Multiply f'(0) * g'(x) 1 * 4 = 4 No, wrong sign
    Add the two slopes cos(4) + 4 = 3.3464 No

    The multiplication is the right shape of the answer, but the two slopes have to be evaluated at the right places. f is not being handed x. It is being handed g(x).

    The real thing

    Two machines, plugged together. The slope of the whole is the product of two slopes - but not just any two.

    We derive it rather than assert it. Let u = g(x) and the whole machine be y = f(u). Take x and nudge it by a small amount h.

    Step 1 - the inner machine. Its input moved by h. A machine with slope g'(x) at x turns that into about g'(x) * h, so u moves by that much.

    Step 2 - the outer machine. Its input, u, has moved by that amount. A machine with slope f'(u) at u turns that into about f'(u) * g'(x) * h of change in its output y.

    Step 3 - divide out the nudge. The total change in y is about f'(u) * g'(x) * h. Divide by h and let it shrink to zero, and the two "about"s collapse into equals:

    slope of f(g(x))  =  f'(g(x)) * g'(x)

    Two things to notice. You must compute u = g(x) before evaluating f', because f' needs to know where it is. And the multiplication is not a coincidence: a change in one thing is fed into the next, so the two factors compose by multiplying.

    The graph view

    The two machines as boxes:

    x  --[ g ]-->  u  --[ f ]-->  y
    • Along the first edge, the slope is g'(x).
    • Along the second edge, the slope is f'(u), with u = g(x).
    • Along the whole path, the slope is the product of the edge slopes.

    The numbers multiply backwards along the edges, output end to input end:

    dy/dx  =  (dy/du) * (du/dx)

    One factor per machine on the path: two machines means two factors, three means three. Write the path down, count the boxes, and you know how many multiplications to do.

    Where this lives in a neural network

    One step of a network:

    x  -->  layer (Wx + b)  -->  activation  -->  next layer  -->  ...  -->  loss

    Ask for the gradient of the loss with respect to one weight W in the first layer. The gradient is the product of the slopes along that path: how much the loss changes when the layer's output changes, times how much the output changes when the activation input changes, times how much the activation input changes when that one weight changes.

    Every factor is a local slope of one small piece - that is the trick of backpropagation: compute each local slope once, then multiply along the path. Chapter 19 does this in code.

    Worked examples

    Example 1 - two machines, agreement check

    f(u) = 3u + 1, g(x) = x^2, at x = 2.

    By algebra. f(g(x)) = 3x^2 + 1. Its slope is 6x. At x = 2: 12.

    By the rule. u = g(2) = 4. The outer machine's slope at u = 4 is f'(4) = 3 (this one happens to be constant, so the point does not matter). The inner machine's slope at x = 2 is g'(2) = 2x = 4. Product: `3 * 4 = 12**.

    Both routes give 12. They must - and that is the point of the exercise.

    Example 2 - where the naive approach fails

    f(u) = sin(u), g(x) = x^2, at x = 2.

    By the rule. u = g(2) = 4, so:

    f'(u) = cos(u) = cos(4) = -0.653644
g'(x) = 2x     = 4
product = 4 * (-0.653644) = -2.614576  ≈  -2.6146

    By algebra, as a check. d/dx sin(x^2) = cos(x^2) * 2x = 4 * cos(4). Same thing.

    Where the naive attempts land. Using f'(x) instead of f'(g(x)) gives cos(2) * 4 = -1.6646. Using f'(0) gives 1 * 4 = 4, the wrong sign entirely. Adding instead of multiplying gives 3.3464.

    All three are wrong, and all three come from the same mistake: treating the slope of f as a property of f rather than of f at the input it received. The outer machine was fed u = 4, never x = 2 and never 0.

    Example 3 - three machines, three factors

    g(x) = x^2, h(u) = 3u + 1, f(v) = 2v, at x = 2. Innermost first: g, then h, then f.

    By algebra. f(h(g(x))) = 2(3x^2 + 1) = 6x^2 + 2. Slope 12x. At x = 2: 24.

    By the rule, one factor per machine. u = g(2) = 4, v = h(4) = 13, f'(v) = 2, h'(u) = 3, g'(x) = 2x = 4. Product: 2 * 3 * 4 = 24.

    Three machines on the path, three factors. Skip a machine and multiply only two slopes, and you get 3 * 4 = 12, exactly half the truth - the missing factor was 2.

    Where this sits in the code

    The contract in labkit/CONTRACT.md gives us two functions for this chapter, both in labkit/calculus.py: compose(f, g) -> callable and chain_grad(f, g, x: float, h: float = 1e-7) -> float.

    chain_grad does not use the chain rule. It measures the slope numerically, the same way derivative from Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/04 - derivatives does - by evaluating the function to the left and right of x and dividing. That is deliberate: if the code applied the rule you are checking, it would only prove the code agrees with itself. As written it is an independent witness. You compute f'(g(x)) * g'(x) with a pencil, and chain_grad tells you what the machine measures.

    The cost: chain_grad evaluates g twice (at x + h and x - h) and f twice on the results - about four calls. A plain derivative calls its function twice, so chain_grad is roughly twice as slow, the price of checking a rule instead of assuming it. The default h = 1e-7 matches derivative's default.

    Your turn - build it

    File: labkit/calculus.py

    Function Signature Must return
    compose compose(f, g) -> callable a callable that takes one argument x and returns f(g(x))
    chain_grad chain_grad(f, g, x: float, h: float = 1e-7) -> float the numeric derivative of f(g(x)) at x

    Both need a docstring: what they do, what they return, what they cost. chain_grad's should mention the several calls to f and g.

    File: labkit/tests/test_calculus.py

    Define local helpers in the test file: def square(x): return x * x, def times_three_plus_one(u): return 3 * u + 1, def times_two(v): return 2 * v. Use math.sin and math.cos for the trig ones.

    The checks, with the numbers you should get:

    1. compose(square, square)(3) returns exactly 9. Use assertEqual.
    2. compose(times_three_plus_one, square)(2) returns exactly 13 (square(2) = 4, then 3 * 4 + 1). Use assertEqual.
    3. chain_grad(times_three_plus_one, square, 2) returns about 12. Use assertAlmostEqual with places=4.
    4. chain_grad(math.sin, square, 2) returns about -2.6146. Use assertAlmostEqual(-2.6145744834, result, places=4).
    5. chain_grad(times_two, compose(times_three_plus_one, square), 2) returns about 24. Use assertAlmostEqual with places=4.

    Check 5 is the three-machine case, and it also tests that compose nests.

    Why assertAlmostEqual and not assertEqual. Binary floats cannot represent most decimals exactly, so 0.1 + 0.2 is not exactly 0.3. More specific to this chapter, chain_grad subtracts two nearly equal numbers and divides by 2e-7, so it lands close to the true slope rather than exactly on it. places=4 means "agree to within half of one ten-thousandth" - loose enough for the arithmetic, tight enough to catch a real mistake.

    Run the checks. From C:\Users\aswat\Projects\laya-try:

    python -m unittest discover -s labkit/tests -v

    Five new tests, and the Chapter 01 tests still passing.

    Hint: the rule is about points, not formulas. Before you multiply anything, write down the number each machine actually receives at the point you are evaluating, and take that machine's slope there.

    Check yourself

    Answer these out loud, in your own words.

    1. In Example 2, what number does f receive, and what is its slope there? What answer would you have reported if you had evaluated that slope at 0 instead?
    2. Why does the rule multiply two slopes instead of adding them? Answer in terms of a small change in x moving through the two machines in turn.
    3. Example 3 stacks three machines. How many factors, what are their values at x = 2, and what would you have got from only two of them?
    4. A 12-layer network with an activation after each layer has one weight in layer 1. Roughly how many slope factors give the gradient of the loss with respect to that weight, and why is that still cheap?
    5. chain_grad is slower than derivative on the same input. How many times does each call its functions, and what is the total for chain_grad?

    Where this goes next

    You now have the rule that makes a network trainable: a slope through a stack of machines is a product of local slopes, one per machine on the path. What you do not have yet is a way to talk about uncertainty - how likely is a given output, and how much the loss wiggles when the inputs wiggle.

    Chapter 07 - Probability, Distributions, and Expectation

    07 · Probability, Distributions, and Expectation

    Part I - The Mathematics

    07 - Probability, Distributions, and Expectation

    How to use this chapter

    Read it once, then build the four functions, run the checks, and answer the five questions out loud. Every number here is checkable with a pencil.

    Build target: labkit/probability.py (4 functions), labkit/tests/test_probability.py (7 checks).

    Why this matters

    Chapters 01 to 06 gave you shapes, derivatives, gradients, and the chain rule - all deterministic: same input, same output.

    What a model hands you is not a number with a known value. It is a number describing how likely each answer is. 0.83 on a ticket classifier does not mean "this ticket is billing-related" - it means "here is how I split my belief across billing, technical, and account, and 0.83 of it is on billing".

    The naive story

    The naive story: a probability is how likely something is, so I take the model's highest number. It breaks in three ways.

    One number hides the spread. 0.83 on billing with 0.17 on one other label is confidence; 0.83 scattered across nine labels is a guess.

    "Most likely" ignores cost. A 0.4 chance of a 10,000 dollar loss and a 0.6 chance of a 100 dollar gain are two facts the naive story cannot hold.

    It treats a model's output as a fact. The model does not know things; it has a belief.

    The real thing

    Probability

    A probability is a number between 0 and 1 describing how likely something is. 0 means it never happens, 1 always, 0.4 means four times in ten. Two rules do most of the work.

    Disjoint outcomes add. A ticket is either billing or technical, never both, so P(billing) + P(technical) = 1. Any outcomes that cannot co-occur have probabilities that add up.

    Independent events multiply. Two events are independent when learning that one happened tells you nothing about the other: a fair coin I do not watch between flips. Then P(A and B) = P(A) * P(B), so two heads in a row is 0.5 * 0.5 = 0.25. If heads made tails more likely, the events would be dependent.

    Random variables

    A random variable is a function that takes an outcome and returns a number. The thing that happens and the number you record are different objects: an agent reads three tickets (the experiment); you record how many were billing-related (the random variable, taking values 0, 1, 2, or 3).

    Distributions

    A discrete distribution is a list of (value, probability) pairs that sums to 1. Suppose each ticket is independently billing-related with probability 0.4. Let X be the number of the next three that are billing-related:

    X (billing tickets out of 3) probability
    0 0.216
    1 0.432
    2 0.288
    3 0.064

    The column sums to 1.000 - the first check on any distribution.

    Expectation

    The expectation is the long-run average:

    E[X] = sum over values v of ( v * P(X = v) )

    Run it a million times and average what you recorded; that is what you get. For the distribution above:

    E[X] = 0*0.216 + 1*0.432 + 2*0.288 + 3*0.064
     = 0 + 0.432 + 0.576 + 0.192
     = 1.200

    One and a fifth tickets, on average - equivalently E[X] = n * p = 1.2.

    The expectation is not the most likely value. Here the most likely value is 1 (probability 0.432), the expectation is 1.200. The expectation is the average outcome; the most likely value is the most common one.

    Variance

    Variance measures spread: how far values typically sit from the mean, as the average of squared deviations.

    Var(X) = E[ (X - E[X])^2 ] = E[X^2] - (E[X])^2

    Variance is in squared units, so people also quote the standard deviation, its square root, in the original units.

    Why spread matters for AI: a model saying "90% confident" promises how often it is right, and that promise is worth something only if the belief is sharp. A model spreading its belief across five answers, none above 0.4, is not 90% confident of anything.

    The binomial distribution

    The binomial distribution counts successes out of n independent trials, each with success probability p:

    P(X = k) = C(n,k) * p^k * (1-p)^(n-k)
    • C(n,k) - the ways to choose which k of the n trials are the successes: n! / (k! * (n-k)!).
    • p^k - the probability those k trials all succeed.
    • (1-p)^(n-k) - the probability the other n-k all fail.

    Pick which trials succeed, then multiply in the probability of that arrangement.

    The normal distribution

    The normal distribution is the bell curve, described by two numbers: the mean mu (the centre) and the standard deviation sigma (the width).

    f(x) = (1 / (sigma * sqrt(2*pi))) * exp(-((x-mu)^2) / (2 * sigma^2))

    f(x) is a density, not a probability. The area under the curve between two points is the probability that X falls between them; the height at a point is not, because height is measured per unit x and probabilities are areas.

    The standard deviation is easy to read off the curve:

    • About 68% of the probability lies within mu ± 1*sigma.
    • About 95% within mu ± 2*sigma.
    • About 99.7% within mu ± 3*sigma.

    That is the 68-95-99.7 rule, a statement about areas.

    The bridge to AI

    A model does not output an answer. It outputs a distribution over answers - a classifier says "billing 0.83, technical 0.11, account 0.06", a language model says "next token ' the' with probability 0.41". Same object.

    So the decision model's whole contract is that this distribution is honest: 0.83 really means it is right about 83% of the time. That is calibration, and it is Chapter 31. Not here; we are only promising you know what question to ask when you get there.

    Worked examples

    Example 1: a six-outcome distribution

    You record how many of five support tickets are billing-related. From history:

    X 0 1 2 3 4 5
    P(X) 0.300 0.250 0.200 0.120 0.080 0.050

    Step 1, check it sums to 1. 0.300 + 0.250 + 0.200 + 0.120 + 0.080 + 0.050 = 1.000.

    Step 2, the expectation. Multiply each value by its probability and add:

    0 * 0.300 = 0.000
1 * 0.250 = 0.250
2 * 0.200 = 0.400
3 * 0.120 = 0.360
4 * 0.080 = 0.320
5 * 0.050 = 0.250
              ------
sum          = 1.580

    Step 3, what the expectation is not. The most likely value is 0 (probability 0.300). The best guess by frequency is 0; 1.580 is the best guess by average error.

    Step 4, the variance as spread. Second moment first:

    E[X^2] = 1^2*0.250 + 2^2*0.200 + 3^2*0.120 + 4^2*0.080 + 5^2*0.050
       = 0.250 + 0.800 + 1.080 + 1.280 + 1.250
       = 4.660

    Then:

    Var(X) = 4.660 - (1.580)^2 = 4.660 - 2.4964 = 2.1636  ≈ 2.164
sd(X)  = sqrt(2.1636) ≈ 1.471

    Example 2: the binomial, n = 3, p = 0.4, exactly 1 success

    P(X = 1) = C(3,1) * 0.4^1 * 0.6^2

    C(3,1) = 3 - three ways to place one success among three trials. 0.4^1 = 0.4, 0.6^2 = 0.36.

    P(X = 1) = 3 * 0.4 * 0.36 = 3 * 0.144 = 0.432

    All four cases, checked against a sum of 1:

    P(X = 0) = C(3,0) * 0.4^0 * 0.6^3 = 1 * 1 * 0.216 = 0.216
P(X = 1) = C(3,1) * 0.4^1 * 0.6^2 = 3 * 0.4 * 0.36  = 0.432
P(X = 2) = C(3,2) * 0.4^2 * 0.6^1 = 3 * 0.16 * 0.6  = 0.288
P(X = 3) = C(3,3) * 0.4^3 * 0.6^0 = 1 * 0.064 * 1   = 0.064

    0.216 + 0.432 + 0.288 + 0.064 = 1.000. Good. The mode is 1 while the expectation is n * p = 1.200, so again the most likely value and the average differ. P(X=0) needs no combination factor: there is one way to fail three times.

    Example 3: the normal distribution

    Density at x = 0 for a standard normal (mu = 0, sigma = 1):

    f(0) = (1 / (1 * sqrt(2*pi))) * exp(0)
     = 1 / 2.506628
     = 0.3989  →  0.399

    The peak height 0.399 is not a probability - the chance of hitting exactly zero is zero for a continuous variable. What 0.399 means: per unit of x, the density near zero is about 0.399. Multiply by a width and you get area, and area is probability.

    The 68-95-99.7 rule as areas. Take mu = 100, sigma = 15:

    • Area from 85 to 115 (mu ± 1*sigma) is about 0.68 - roughly 68 of every 100 draws land in that window.
    • Area from 70 to 130 (mu ± 2*sigma) is about 0.95.
    • Area from 55 to 145 (mu ± 3*sigma) is about 0.997.

    What the standard deviation means, spelled out: sigma is the typical distance from the mean to a value you would actually see. With sigma = 15, a value is usually within about 15 of 100; twice that catches 95% of values; three times catches essentially all.

    Where this sits in the code

    You create labkit/probability.py with four functions, matching labkit/CONTRACT.md.

    Function Signature Behaviour
    mean mean(xs: list[float]) -> float arithmetic mean; ValueError on empty input
    variance variance(xs: list[float]) -> float population variance, mean of squared deviations from the mean
    binomial_pmf binomial_pmf(n: int, p: float, k: int) -> float probability of exactly k successes in n trials
    normal_pdf normal_pdf(x: float, mu: float = 0.0, sigma: float = 1.0) -> float density of the normal at x
    • mean returns sum(xs) / len(xs). Empty input has no mean, so raise ValueError.
    • variance is the population variance: divide by len(xs), not len(xs) - 1. It is mean((x - m)^2 for x in xs).
    • binomial_pmf computes C(n,k) * p^k * (1-p)^(n-k). Build C(n,k) multiplicatively: C(n,k) = C(n,k-1) * (n-k+1) / k.
    • normal_pdf returns a density, not a probability. Defaults mu = 0.0, sigma = 1.0 give the standard normal.

    Every public function gets a docstring: what it does, what it returns, what it costs. Tests go in labkit/tests/test_probability.py.

    Your turn - build it

    File: labkit/probability.py, four functions with the signatures and behaviour above.

    Checks: labkit/tests/test_probability.py. Seven of them:

    # Call Expected
    1 mean([1, 2, 3, 4]) 2.5
    2 mean([1.5]) 1.5
    3 mean([]) raises ValueError
    4 variance([1, 2, 3, 4]) 1.25
    5 variance([2, 2, 2, 2]) 0.0
    6 binomial_pmf(3, 0.4, 1) 0.432
    7 normal_pdf(0.0) 0.3989 (about 0.3989422804)

    Check 4 by hand first: deviations from the mean 2.5 are -1.5, -0.5, 0.5, 1.5, squares 2.25, 0.25, 0.25, 2.25, sum 5.0, so 5.0 / 4 = 1.25. Divide by 4, not 3.

    Use assertAlmostEqual, not assertEqual, for float results. Binary floating point cannot represent most decimals exactly, so a computed 0.432 may be 0.43199999999999994. assertAlmostEqual(a, b, places=3) compares within a tolerance - the only honest way to compare two computed approximations. Check 3 uses assertRaises(ValueError).

    Run the checks. From C:\Users\aswat\Projects\laya-try:

    python -m unittest discover -s labkit/tests -v

    Hint: the binomial coefficient does not need a factorial if you build it one step at a time - each new k is reachable from the last by multiplying and dividing.

    Check yourself

    Where this goes next

    Chapter 08 - Entropy, Cross-Entropy, and KL Divergence - see Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/08 - information-theory.

    08 · Entropy, Cross-Entropy, and KL Divergence

    Part I - The Mathematics

    08 - Entropy, Cross-Entropy, and KL Divergence

    How to use this chapter

    Read it once with a pencil, build three functions in labkit/probability.py, run the checks, and answer the five questions at the end out loud. Every number here is small enough to verify by hand; if one does not reproduce, fix it first.

    Build target: labkit/probability.py (entropy, cross_entropy, kl_divergence), labkit/tests/test_probability.py (6 checks).

    Why this matters

    Every model in this book gets a score. That score is a loss, and this chapter is where the loss comes from.

    Chapter 12 names the loss and Chapter 13 runs the training loop, but both need a number to push against. The loss of every language model ever trained is a cross entropy; the gap between how confident a model was and how confident it should have been is a KL divergence.

    The naive story

    The intuitive version: a model makes predictions, some right and some wrong, so the loss should be small when right and large when wrong - it counts mistakes.

    That breaks in one place. A model that says "90% sure, and the truth was the other 10%" and one that says "51% sure, and the truth was the other 49%" have both made exactly one mistake, so a mistake-counting loss charges them the same. But surprise depends on what your beliefs said, so being wrong while certain is a different kind of failure from being wrong while unsure, and the loss must be able to tell them apart.

    The real thing

    Surprise: how much an event tells you

    Define the surprise of an event with probability p as

    surprise(p) = -ln(p)

    in units called nats: "how many nats of surprise does this event carry?"

    • p = 1: -ln(1) = 0 nats. Nothing surprising happened.
    • p = 0.5: -ln(0.5) = 0.693 nats.
    • p = 0.01: -ln(0.01) = 4.605 nats.

    A rare event tells you more than a likely one: if you already expected it, it added nothing; if you did not, you learned something.

    Why the logarithm? Because independent surprises must add: ln(x) + ln(y) = ln(x*y) turns multiplication into addition, and addition is what a running total needs. Base e is our convention; base 2 gives the same ideas in bits.

    Entropy: average surprise

    A distribution is a list of probabilities summing to 1. Weight each outcome's surprise by how likely it is and average:

    H(p) = -sum over i of  p[i] * ln(p[i])

    The fair coin, p = [0.5, 0.5]: -[0.5 * (-0.693) + 0.5 * (-0.693)] = 0.693 nats. All mass on one outcome, p = [1.0, 0.0]: -[1.0 * 0 + 0 * (skipped)] = 0 nats.

    Read entropy as "how uncertain is this distribution?" The fair coin is maximally uncertain; the certain outcome is not uncertain at all. So a model with a spread-out output distribution is uncertain, and one concentrated on a single token is sure - a property of the distribution, right or wrong.

    Cross entropy: the loss

    Let p be the truth and q be what the model believed:

    H(p, q) = -sum over i of  p[i] * ln(q[i])

    Read it out loud: "the average surprise of the truth, under the model's beliefs." The truth is what happened; the model's beliefs are what it is scored against.

    If q agrees with p, cross entropy collapses to plain entropy H(p) - the best any distribution can do on that truth. And if q puts tiny probability on the true outcome, the loss explodes: against the truth p = [1, 0], the models q = [0.9, 0.1] and q = [0.1, 0.9] pay 0.105 and 2.303 nats for the identical mistake (Example 2).

    The penalty for confident mistakes

    Take a model that puts confidence c on its answer. If it is right it pays -ln(c); if it is wrong, the truth sat at probability 1-c in its head, so it pays -ln(1-c).

    A model that is 99% sure and wrong pays 4.605 nats; the same mistake at 51% confidence costs 0.713 nats, and being right at 51% confidence costs 0.673 nats. When you are barely sure, the loss barely cares which way it goes. The full table is Example 3.

    So the loss punishes confidence more than ignorance. Being wrong when you knew you were guessing is cheap; being wrong when you insisted you were right is expensive - deliberately, because a confidently wrong model is not corrected by data it disagrees with.

    KL divergence: the gap between belief and truth

    Cross entropy mixes two things: how uncertain the truth is, and how far the model is from it. Subtract the first to keep only the second:

    KL(p || q) = sum over i of  p[i] * ln(p[i] / q[i])
           = H(p, q) - H(p)

    That is the extra nats you pay purely because your beliefs differ from the truth. It is zero when the two distributions agree, and never negative.

    KL is not symmetric, and the direction matters. KL(p || q) means "truth p measured against belief q" - the average surprise of the truth under q. Swapping the arguments asks a different question:

    KL([0.9, 0.1] || [0.5, 0.5]) = 0.368
KL([0.5, 0.5] || [0.9, 0.1]) = 0.511

    The first asks "if the truth is 90/10, how much does believing 50/50 cost you?"; the second asks the reverse. The first is the one that matters for training, because the truth is what is predicted and the beliefs are what is adjusted.

    Why this trains every language model

    One sentence: predicting the next token is cross entropy between the true next token and the model's distribution over the vocabulary.

    Not an analogy - literally the loss function. The truth p is one-hot (all mass on the token that actually came next), the model's q is its softmax over the vocabulary, the loss is -ln(q[correct_token]), and training means moving the weights until that number falls. Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/28 - next-token-training makes this concrete; Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/29 - sampling picks tokens from the distribution the model learned.

    A preview of what breaks

    Computing cross entropy needs a softmax over logits, and a naive softmax in floating point breaks: exp(1000) overflows to infinity, infinity over infinity gives nan, and one nan poisons the gradient. Real implementations subtract the maximum logit before exponentiating and use a log-sum-exp trick.

    That is Learning/Foundations/Book 1 - Foundations/09 - numerics. This chapter does the mathematics in clean decimals, because the mathematics is what tells you what the fix must preserve.

    Worked examples

    Example 1 - entropy of a few distributions

    All by hand, all with ln:

    Distribution -sum p[i] ln p[i] Entropy (nats)
    [1.0, 0.0] -[1.0 * 0 + 0 * (skip)] 0.000
    [0.99, 0.01] -[0.99 * (-0.010) + 0.01 * (-4.605)] 0.056
    [0.9, 0.1] -[0.9 * (-0.105) + 0.1 * (-2.303)] 0.325
    [0.6, 0.4] -[0.6 * (-0.511) + 0.4 * (-0.916)] 0.673
    [0.5, 0.5] -[0.5 * (-0.693) + 0.5 * (-0.693)] 0.693
    [1/3, 1/3, 1/3] -3 * (1/3) * ln(1/3) 1.099

    The last row in full: ln(1/3) = -1.099, so -3 * (1/3) * (-1.099) = 1.099 nats. As the mass concentrates, entropy falls: 0.693 -> 0.325 -> 0.056 -> 0.

    Example 2 - cross entropy against a certain truth

    Truth p = [1.0, 0.0], so every term with p[i] = 0 drops out.

    Against q = [0.9, 0.1]:

    H(p, q) = -[1.0 * ln(0.9) + 0.0 * ln(0.1)]
        = -[1.0 * (-0.105) + 0]
        = 0.105 nats

    Against q = [0.1, 0.9]:

    H(p, q) = -[1.0 * ln(0.1) + 0.0 * ln(0.9)]
        = -[1.0 * (-2.303) + 0]
        = 2.303 nats

    The second costs 22 times as much, for a model that was only 0.8 less confident.

    Example 3 - the confident-wrong table

    The truth is outcome 2; the model was c confident about outcome 1, so the truth sat at 1 - c in its head:

    c truth's probability loss if wrong: -ln(1-c) loss if right: -ln(c)
    0.99 0.01 4.605 0.010
    0.90 0.10 2.303 0.105
    0.60 0.40 0.916 0.511
    0.51 0.49 0.713 0.673

    At 99% confidence, right and wrong differ by a factor of 458. At 51% confidence they differ by 1.06.

    Example 4 - KL by hand

    p = [0.9, 0.1], q = [0.5, 0.5]:

    KL(p || q) = 0.9 * ln(0.9/0.5) + 0.1 * ln(0.1/0.5)
           = 0.9 * 0.588 + 0.1 * (-1.609)
           = 0.368

    The identity agrees: H(p, q) = 0.693, H(p) = 0.325, 0.693 - 0.325 = 0.368.

    Where this sits in the code

    labkit/probability.py, which Chapter 07 starts. This chapter adds three functions, per labkit/CONTRACT.md:

    Function Signature Behaviour
    entropy entropy(p: list[float]) -> float -sum(p[i] * ln(p[i])), in nats
    cross_entropy cross_entropy(p: list[float], q: list[float]) -> float -sum(p[i] * ln(q[i])); ValueError if the lengths differ
    kl_divergence kl_divergence(p: list[float], q: list[float]) -> float sum(p[i] * ln(p[i] / q[i]))

    Three conventions:

    1. Natural logarithms. Use math.log, not math.log2. Results are in nats; base 2 would put every number here off by a factor of ln(2) = 0.693.
    2. A zero term contributes 0. Skip terms whose leading probability is 0 rather than asking Python for ln(0): p[i] == 0 in entropy, and q[i] == 0 in cross_entropy and kl_divergence. (Mathematically that means infinite loss; the contract keeps it finite so every number stays checkable with a pencil.)
    3. Length mismatches raise ValueError, before any arithmetic happens, so a mismatch fails loudly instead of truncating.

    Tests go in labkit/tests/test_probability.py, using unittest, like labkit/tests/test_linalg.py.

    Your turn - build it

    File 1: labkit/probability.py

    Write the bodies for entropy(p), cross_entropy(p, q) and kl_divergence(p, q) - signatures and behaviours above: natural log, zero terms contribute 0, ValueError on length mismatch. Give each a docstring: what it does, returns, and costs.

    File 2: labkit/tests/test_probability.py

    Six checks; the expected values are here to six decimals:

    1. entropy([0.5, 0.5]) is 0.693147
    2. entropy([1.0, 0.0]) is 0.0 (all the mass on one outcome, so no uncertainty)
    3. entropy([0.9, 0.1]) is 0.325083
    4. cross_entropy([1.0, 0.0], [0.9, 0.1]) is 0.105361
    5. cross_entropy([1.0, 0.0], [0.1, 0.9]) is 2.302585
    6. kl_divergence([0.9, 0.1], [0.5, 0.5]) is 0.368064

    Add a seventh for the shape error: cross_entropy([1.0, 0.0], [0.5, 0.5, 0.0]) must raise ValueError - use assertRaises, the way test_linalg.py does.

    Use assertAlmostEqual, not assertEqual. The expected values are rounded to six decimals and the true values are irrational (ln(0.9) has no exact decimal form), so == fails even with perfect code. assertAlmostEqual compares within a tolerance - "the same number to within floating-point error". The default tolerance is 7 decimal places; assertAlmostEqual(actual, 0.693147, places=6) states yours.

    Hint: all three quantities are the same shape - a sum of probability x ln(ratio) terms - differing only in which probability sits in front and what the ratio is. Write one term carefully and the rest follows.

    3. Truth is p = [1.0, 0.0]. A model says q = [0.9, 0.1] and pays 0.105 nats. What does that measure? What does it pay with q = [0.1, 0.9], and why is the second so much bigger when the mistake is identical?
